@@135711717 <p> That 's not something we hear routinely in news bulletins , of course . But such numeric snapshots - what 's up , what 's down and overall trends - are very familiar from daily reports of everything from stock markets to sports . <p> Might there be an argument for applying the same format to planetary health ? Some media organisations already think so , including updates on atmospheric carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) levels in their regular coverage . But the practice remains far from mainstream . <p> It makes sense for news outlets to report this way , however , because humans understand trends better than abstractions or hard-to-visualise phenomena . <p> A brief summary of share price movements , for example , may not be the full financial story . But it does provide a regular barometer of likely changes to things that affect us - like fuel prices , mortgage payments or retirement savings . <p> The data is often easily available to news outlets , easy to visualise graphically , simple to slot in alongside weather and sport , and audiences are used @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ issue 's visibility influences public opinion and government attention . Numbers crystallise this " agenda-setting " process , prompting questions about why those numbers are rising or falling , which policies influence them , and who is responsible . <p> In other words , what gets reported and how it 's reported matter . Societies prioritise what they notice most , and they can manage what they measure . <h> Connecting climate to everyday life <p> The fact we have n't ritualised the reporting of atmospheric carbon readings - a key measure of global warming - is n't because we lack data . <p> It might be argued such numbers are n't as relevant to people 's everyday lives as interest rates and stock markets . But that 's increasingly not the case . Environmental statistics help track changes that do and will affect us . <p> Links between climate change and extreme weather , rising insurance costs , transport disruptions and food prices are intricate and changeable . Daily atmospheric CO2 reports compress the complexity of a multifaceted problem into something we can grasp more readily . <p> There @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ news " outlets doing just this : Reasons to be Cheerful ( founded by artist and musician David Byrne ) , Positive News and Fix the News report numbers related to tangible initiatives such as new hectares of forest reserve or revived populations of threatened species . <h> Normalising environmental awareness <p> To help prevent people tuning out repetitive data that changes slowly , reporting can frame the numbers in different ways - how fast they 're moving compared with past decades , the distance from specific carbon budget goals , and whether they 're moving faster or slower than predicted . <p> Contextual stories can connect the data to regional consequences and human stories of local climate action success . That casts the CO2 updates as indexes of active response rather than passive observation . <p> For public broadcasters with mandates or charters to provide public interest journalism , the fit is obvious . <p> Regular CO2 news would also balance the default reporting of economic indicators that can be perceived as prioritising markets over ecosystems . Presenting environmental numbers in the same way helps normalise attention to ecological @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hot-button issues like the cost of living and healthcare , climate awareness becomes less about ideology or " climate wars " and more about the practical challenges of maintaining a habitable planet . 
@@135711851 <h> These Waikato hydro lakes are supposed to be safe to swim in , but a toxic algae problem is getting worse <p> In a finger-like branch of Lake Ohakuri the water is as green as the grassy paddocks surrounding it . <p> It 's thick with algae . The microscopic plant-like organisms lack leaves or roots , but possess a prodigious ability to feast on nutrients and with the help of sunlight , multiply exponentially , turning clear water into a murky , and sometimes toxic , spinach soup . <p> As unappealing as the water looks , today is a " good " day . On bad days , the algae clump together like old friends embracing , creating snot-like mats of slime . On really bad days , there 's a stench of rot and death . <p> Despite a legislated vision for the water to be safe to swim in and talks starting more than a decade ago to reduce nutrients in the river , summer algal blooms plague Lake Ohakuri and other hydro lakes along the Waikato River . <p> Swimming in this water is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ acre block on the Whirinaki Arm of the lake . It was intended to be a summer bolthole , but the water conditions here are frequently poor . <p> A pontoon moored off the shore bobs hopefully in the green water . It was built to support summers of fun , but there are days when the pontoon stays dry . Withers swims here sometimes , but not on the days when it 's radioactive green , or when the snot clumps have formed . <p> Yes , sometimes the water gives him a sore throat , he says , but he likes to think he judges the conditions well enough to not get seriously ill . He 's careful about not putting his head under . <p> He 's far more cautious when it comes to his children ; they are often banned from entering the river completely . Algae can produce toxins which attack the liver . <p> Peter Withers Photo : RNZ / Farah Hancock <p> Leanne Archer lives further around the lake . She describes the water as often neon-green , and sometimes smelling of rot @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but the potentially toxic algal blooms means she keeps her distance . Her dog Misty loves to swim , but has to be kept inside . The few times she escaped and played in the water she became sick , " vomiting and vomiting , " says Archer . <p> Katrin Halbert is another Lake Ohakuri local with a dog . On its worst days she says the Whirinaki Arm of the lake is fluorescent green . " You know , The Hulk , The Incredible Hulk , when it turns green , it 's like that . " Last year one of her dogs drank from the lake and within half an hour started vomiting . The days of walking her dogs in the reserve are over , she says . <p> Leanne Archer and Katrin Halbert Photo : RNZ / Cole Eastham-Farrelly <p> For dogs , cyanobacteria can be lethal even when the toxins are at a level below what would trigger a public health warning . <p> There 's a bevy of organisations playing a part in the Waikato River . <p> Mercury Energy runs the hydro @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Waikato Regional Council grants resource consents and sets the rules for land and nutrient use in the catchment . The Waikato River Authority is responsible for the legislated vision of a river that is safe to swim in . Joining them are property owners dotted along the river including farms , forests and industry . <p> Toxic algae can be fatal for dogs . Misty became sick after brief exposure to the water . Photo : Supplied <p> All the big players are aware of the algal blooms , but each says it 's following the rules . An algal bloom working group they belong to has met for the past three years , but frustrated locals are n't seeing any concrete action . They have taken to documenting lake conditions on a public Facebook page . It 's littered with images of sunny days and lurid green water . <h> The toxic problem <p> Before the Waikato River was dammed , a drop of water could travel its 425 kilometre length in seven days . Today that same drop , beginning in the clear waters of Lake Taupo @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ By the time it reaches Port Waikato it is murky brown , and the journey has stretched into weeks . <p> The eight hydro lakes along the river act as batteries for Mercury Energy , holding water to generate power and producing around 10 percent of New Zealand 's electricity . <p> There are eight dams along the Waikato River Photo : RNZ <p> Some locals think the dams and how water levels are managed contribute to conditions conducive to algal blooms . Others point to weed spraying and say the nutrients released as the weeds in the lake die , turbo charge algae growth . <p> Some blame a continual flow of nutrients like nitrogen and phosphorus , seeping in from the farms , horticulture and industry in the area . Climate change gets a mention too , warm weather and tempestuous summer storms can sweep even more nutrients from land into the river system , providing a banquet for algae growth . <p> Algae are n't necessarily bad ; they 're an important part of freshwater ecosystems , providing food for the invertebrates that fish feed on . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ combined with an abundant supply of nutrients can lead to population booms known as blooms . <p> The boom turns to a bust when conditions change . This might be a drop in temperature or when the algae have consumed all the nutrients in the water . As the algae decomposes its cells collapse and cyanotoxins can be released . <p> Not all types of algae produce toxins , but the ones that do can make the water poisonous , triggering asthma and hayfever , skin rashes , stomach upsets , tingling around the mouth , headaches , breathing difficulties and visual problems . <p> Worryingly , data from the Waikato Regional Council shows the proportion of cyanobacteria ( the type of algae that is toxic ) in the hydro lakes it monitors is slowly increasing during summer months and becoming the predominant algae . <p> It 's the amount of cyanobacteria present which can trigger a public health alert . <p> Photo : Upper Waikato Algal Blooms Working Group <p> At Lake Arapuni , one of the last hydro lakes on the river , Ryan Fynn keeps his dog @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ never allowed near the water when it looks green . <p> His first memory of the lake was as a four-year-old , standing on the front of his uncle 's water skis . Back then they piped the water from the lake straight to their house as drinking water . Now , it 's a different story . <p> Some days , when the wind blows in the right direction it drags a scent from the lake , which he describes as " sewagey " , into his windows . This summer the algae was " like a big , thick mat " his boat struggled to get through . <p> Ryan Fynn Photo : RNZ / Cole Eastham-Farrelly <p> He 's battled to get satisfactory answers from the Waikato Regional Council . " They just fob everyone off . " <p> Fynn thinks part of the problem stems from how long water is held in the lakes by Mercury Energy , the company which runs the hydro dams . The longer it 's held , the longer the algae has a chance to grow , he suggests . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ looks clear , but a sign warns there are high levels of cyanobacteria . <p> It 's the same sign that was seen by RNZ on a sunny Sunday in January . That day , the water also looked clear and children splashed happily next to the sign . <h> Keeping people safe <p> Data does n't lie , but the data Waikato Regional Council uses to assess whether the water is poisonous does n't necessarily give a full picture of what 's going on day-to-day . <p> Toxic conditions could occur more often than what has been recorded without triggering public health warnings and sometimes health warnings remain in place when water conditions have improved . <p> Only four lakes ( Ohakuri , Maraetai , Karapiro and more recently Arapuni ) are tested for cyanobacteria once a month between November and April . Tests are done on a Monday and results come back Wednesday . If toxin levels exceed safe recreational thresholds signs are put up , and a public health warning is given . Testing is supposed to increase to at least every seven days . <p> Weekly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ four lakes , but this measures a pigment only found in cyanobacteria , not the toxin itself . The council is yet to work out how to use this result to estimate whether toxin levels breach guidelines . <p> Separate state of the environment monitoring is done at six points along the river . These monthly samples do n't test for cyanobacteria , but do test for nutrient levels such as nitrogen and phosphorus , which are nutrients algae feed on . <p> Rob Dexter is part of Let 's Be Clear , a charity aiming to help improve water quality . He has reservations about what can be learnt from snapshots in time like this . <p> The way he describes it , monthly cyanobacteria testing is like testing a person with diabetes once a month and saying those readings are indicative of their daily blood sugar levels . <p> When it comes to human health , he 's doubtful the current system of infrequent testing with a 48 hour turnaround time for results - then another lag before warning signs are erected - is adequate . Blooms may @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ moved further down river . <p> While technology for continuously monitoring cyanobacteria does n't exist in a cost-effective way , he argues " surrogates " can be used . Combined with weather and flow , the likelihood of a bloom could be modelled . When cloud cover is n't an issue satellite images can also show blooms from space , which he says could be used in conjunction with webcam images . <p> Dexter has installed real time monitoring systems on private properties along the river at his own cost . They have shown spikes of nitrogen entering the water after major storms , such as Cyclone Gabrielle . An algal bloom occurred in the weeks after the storm . <p> He believes resource consents should be evaluated against Te Ture Whaimana o Te Awa o Waikato , the legislated vision and strategy for the Waikato River , established as part of a Treaty settlement . The vision calls for water that is safe to swim in and take food from along its entire length . <p> " I believe almost every decision on the Waikato River is being made @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the intent that it 's been used for . " <h> Institutions are following the rules , but are the rules fit for purpose ? <p> Mercury Energy , the Waikato Regional Council and the Waikato River Authority have defined roles and rules , and while organisations say they are meeting them , this is not stopping the ongoing appearance of blooms . <p> Emily Collis is the operations lead at Mercury Energy . The company is aware of the algal blooms that plague the hydro lakes but she does n't accept that the power company is part of the problem . <p> " At this stage , there 's no evidence to suggest that the way we 're managing the river is contributing . " <p> The company repeatedly told RNZ it operates within the rules of the resource consents that dictate the amount of flow and the lake levels . Its contractual agreement to supply electricity also plays a part in the way it manages the lakes . <p> Collis has heard the suggestions that flushing the lakes will clear blooms , but does n't feel that would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ any particular studies that we would then go and share or publish or anything like that . But because we are active on the river every day , we do have observations that tend us to believe that even if we were in high flow conditions , a lot of the algae blooms will not move simply because of their location in those slower moving areas , " she says . <p> The spraying of diquat to kill weeds that can clog the dam turbines is a sore point for locals , who say they notice blooms after spraying . <p> Mercury says it has used diquat for 17 years and algal blooms have not always occurred after its use . <p> Research completed by NIWA , ( now Earth Sciences New Zealand ) showed weeds release a large amount of nutrients when they die . It suggested more research was needed to understand the effects in a lake system . When asked if Mercury would fund research into this Collis indicated the company was " open to opportunities " . <p> Is she at all concerned the company will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Mercury has a hydro stakeholder manager who is often in the community helping people understand Mercury 's role in the river , she says . The company also tries to support communities with funds and sponsorships of the Waikato River Trail . <p> Mercury points to Waikato Regional Council as the agency responsible for the management of the river 's water quality . <p> The council 's science manager Mike Scarsbrook says the organisation is worried about the changes seen in the river . He says the blooms are becoming more frequent and more severe . <p> " It 's not a good space for us to be in . We are working towards improving the health and wellbeing of the river , but we 're certainly seeing worrying signs . " <p> Climate change plays a role , but the blooms also need nutrients to grow , he says . This can come from nitrogen and phosphate washing in from farms , or other enterprises dotted along the river . <p> The council is proposing significant changes to how the land surrounding the river is used , which include @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plan change 1 is the council 's answer to meeting Te Ture Whaimana o Te Awa o Waikato 's legislated vision of water that is safe for swimming and food gathering . <p> The plan change was first notified in 2016 and applies to approximately 10,000 properties and covers more than a million hectares within the Waikato and Waipa river catchments . Over a decade on , it 's still not enacted . Currently it sits with the Environment Court , caught up in a slew of appeals , although interim decisions made by the court look promising for the plan 's future . <p> If it does come into effect , change wo n't happen overnight . Improvement has been given an 80-year runway , meaning the reduction in nutrient levels in the river is n't expected to be reached until 2096 . <p> Not everybody thinks the reductions will have an effect . Former Fish and Game water scientist Adam Canning previously told RNZ the nutrient reduction targets in the plan were watered down to a level he described as maintaining the " status quo " . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to achieve it for 80 years . 2096 . I 'll be dead . " <p> Canning does n't believe the reductions will ensure the water is safe for swimming and food gathering , achieving the legislated vision of Te Ture Whaimana o Te Awa o Waikato . <p> Chief executive of the Waikato River Authority Antoine Coffin is the man in charge of Te Ture Whaimana o Te Awa o Waikato . The Authority has iwi and Crown representatives on its board and distributes funding for restoration projects . It sets the vision for the health of the Waikato River . <p> " We 've been set up as a voice for the river , " says Coffin . <p> But having a voice is n't the same as having teeth . The authority 's vision sits above other legislation , such as the Resource Management Act , but the authority does n't have enforcement powers . It ca n't compel Mercury Energy to increase water flow to see if that affects blooms , demand the council 's new plan be put in place before the Environment Court process @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on fertiliser . <p> Asked whether the Waikato River Authority was taking a leadership role on algal blooms , Coffin does not directly answer , but says long-term solutions , such as the council 's new plan are important . He does n't think the plan will fix everything , " but it 's a good start " . <p> Continuing algal blooms , " would be an antithesis to the vision and a strategy for the river , " he says . <p> The group met twice in 2024 and once in 2025 and 2026 . Its aim is to reach an understanding of the drivers of the algal blooms and , according to a memo from a September 2025 meeting , " to advance a Response Plan for managing the impacts of harmful algal blooms in the Upper Waikato - within the context of existing interventions to manage water quality , such as the Waikato Regional Council Proposed Plan Change 1 , and Te Ture Whaimana o awa o Waikato . " <p> One of the things the group is working on is communication . " Together , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , in particular , what specific language will help people to heed those risks - we understand that many people still use the water even when health warnings are in place , " a council statement said . <p> Coffin is confident the working group is more than a box-ticking exercise but says getting alignment between the various groups , each with their own mandate , is a " conundrum " . <h> Community takes control <p> For locals , the stance of the big players , ranging from denying any impact , to having their hands tied by rules , offers little comfort . They 're yet to see any improvement as a result of the working group . <p> Hope Woodward is a councillor for the Mangakino-Pouakani ward of the Taupo District Council . <p> " Heartbreaking , " is a term she uses repeatedly about the water condition , and she 's agitating for action . <p> She 's attended working group meetings and suggested a survey be conducted to understand how the water quality affects the community . The Waikato Regional Council agreed to her idea @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would be done the council 's response was , " in the next fiscal year , funding dependent " . <p> She set up a survey herself using an online tool that cost $50 . Eighty-one of the almost 100 people who completed it said poor water quality stopped activities . At least 41 percent of those who responded to the survey said they or their pets had been sick after being in contact with the water . <p> Almost half of the comments touched on perceived inaction and fragmented responsibility . <p> " There seems to be nothing happening , we complained two years ago and nothing changed , " one respondent wrote . <p> " Somebody needs to take accountability and fix it , " another commented . <p> " I think the responsible entities just need to stop having all these discussions that have no resolutions whatsoever . It just seems that nobody wants to take any accountability for what 's gone on . " <p> She wants farming consents to be checked , and she 's keen for more research to be done into the effect @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't identify a single thing being done now that will stop another summer of blooms . <p> " There 's a lot of data gathering and discussion happening , which is important , however we now need to see that translate into real , on the ground action . " <p> The working group 's eventual response plan will only satisfy the community if it 's backed by accountability and delivery , she says . <p> Karl Hitchcock Photo : RNZ / Cole Eastham-Farrelly <p> At Lake Maraetai in Mangakino , Karl Hitchcock says the blooms have gotten worse since he bought his property . <p> " We 've got pictures of the lake . It 's just glowing in the dark . It looks radioactive . " <p> It makes wakeboarding and swimming unsafe and there 's a flow-on effect for the community . Businesses which rely on the weekend recreational visitors are struggling , people just do n't bother coming anymore . There 's only one upside , he jokes . " It 's good for the swimming pool , because all the locals will swim in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of the key voices on the community Facebook page encouraging people to send in photos of water conditions and looking for opportunities to improve the situation . <p> Hitchcock has looked into whether a fund administered by the Waikato Regional Council might be applied to pay for webcams at the lakes , but was told it probably was n't the right fit for that particular fund . He had hoped giving the ability for people to see whether the water was pea soup green , or covered in scum might prevent wasted trips to the lake . <p> The council told RNZ webcams are one of the options the working group is considering , along with drone footage , community reporting via photo and satellite imagery . <p> He 's now planning to apply for funding from Mercury Energy to try an ultrasonic treatment for the lakes where blooms occur . This would consist of a solar-powered floating unit that emits sound waves that kill algae . <p> Without some concrete action he thinks next summer , " will only be worse " . <p> The river is an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ power generation capability . " <p> He 's another fan of more data being gathered to help drive decisions about how to improve the water quality . <p> " When the water comes out of the Taupo gates , it 's crystal , it 's pristine , it 's so good up there . @@135745619 <p> All of the excerpts in this essay are from Wehm 's novel The Department of What It ( Really ) Means to be Human . <p> The cicadas are singing to me as I sit at my keyboard , a soundtrack that plays off and on throughout summer . A summer that reminds me more of the time I spent living in the hot , humid tropics than the temperate Te Whanganui-a-Tara Wellington I found when I arrived here 15 years ago . At least we still have the wind . Wellington is comfortable enough , even now . <p> We 're lucky here . We 'll be comfortable longer than many other places will , but I do n't really like being sticky . Our proximity to the ever-encroaching sea worries me , too . I have a recurring stress dream about watching a tsunami roll over the airport and subsume Evans Bay , but I know that 's not the more likely danger . Incremental change is what gets us , the apocryphal boiling frogs . I ca n't help but wonder what it will be like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we 'll find ways to push back the wave , make lemonade from the lemons . <p> I like to write fiction about a future that has at least a kernel of hope within it . It forces me to imagine better-case scenarios , which is n't necessarily my first instinct . <p> The classic old villa had been saved by the damming of the harbour mouth , and the five of them worked hard to keep it up . It was a beautiful building and the nearby beach was great for swimming on the days the wind was n't up . <p> Climate change is n't the point of that story . It 's just there in the background , a part of the landscape . <p> These are perilous times for working writers , but one thing remains true : writing is resilient . You can do it anywhere , with minimal equipment . I 've worked on books on lunch breaks at an office job , in the galley of a boat in a heaving sea , on a beach under a straw umbrella , in a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you can write . It 's not like fishing or landscaping or working a food truck , where the answer to " what 's it like out " makes the difference between getting paid or not . <p> In addition to my solo work , I often collaborate with other authors . I 'm currently a part of a five-person team called Darkly Lem and for the past few years we 've met online to write together a couple of times a week . Recently , I did n't see the face of one of my co-writers in months , because his camera shuts off when it overheats . Another member of that team has missed several meetings due to surprise childcare duties on the increasing number of snow days . They apparently just do n't have the infrastructure to handle so much snow in North Carolina . Who would have guessed ? <p> Here at home , the start of my friend 's residency is postponed due to a storm that means half of Wellington ca n't get into town . We 'll celebrate some other day , instead @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transport staff who ca n't work , or the commuters who ca n't work from home . We 're resilient . <p> Anya padded into the common room in shorts and a singlet , her feet bare . This was the hottest summer on record according to Metservice , the temperatures well into the thirties most days . <p> Climate change is n't something we think about when my colleagues and I plan our work . It 's just there , in our chit-chat about the weather , driving the storms that knock out the internet , influencing how we manage our days . <p> Being drawn to dystopia is natural in difficult times . But to my mind , at this exact moment in history it feels liberating , even radical , to imagine a better future instead . I find it easy -- rational , even -- to be pessimistic , but if you ca n't picture the world in which you want to live , you ca n't build it . The future is a house we are building together , and it needs architects as well @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the tools we use to create the future . I think about the future I would create for myself and my community and I tell myself the story of what I 'd like my life to be . Knowing my interests , it will probably be science fiction . But even if the world I most would love to live in ca n't exist in my own lifetime , in telling myself the story I 'll learn what I want from the world that exists now . <p> After all , knowing what you want is the first step to making it happen . <p> Outside , the afternoon sun was bright and the air was hot . They were aware of all the people around them , the noise of the city . The world felt suddenly large and full of possibilities , positive and negative . Em and Anya walked towards the waterfront , watching as a sea cleaner made its ponderous way toward the lock at the harbour mouth . <p> ' I tell myself the story of what I 'd like my life to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ There 's this old chestnut where " talking about the weather " is shorthand for the most dull , banal conversation a person can imagine . Do kids these days still think that ? I do n't even think I think that , and I 'm old enough to use the phrase " kids these days . " <p> When I was a sailor I was , appropriately , obsessed with the weather . I always knew what the forecast was , the direction and force of the wind , the size and period of the ocean swell . When I met up with other folks on the water , we all talked about the weather . It was the most interesting topic going . When I moved ashore it took years to lose that habit , but now I feel the old itch to know coming back . The days are n't predictable anymore and , even in my comfortable city , in my comfortable life , I 'm not isolated from the changes happening all over the world . <p> Our choices and their consequences are always there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , like the cicadas outside my window . I thought the storm would put an end to their song , but no . The next day they are still there , screaming away . They are resilient , too . <p> I wonder what their song will be like decades from now . Will they change or will we ? <p> The Department of What It ( Really ) Means to be Human by M. @@135745691 <h> Ancient warning of climate calamity <p> Lidar has stripped away the forest to reveal Angkor as it was . In the centre is the city visited by Zhou Daguan in 1296 . Two reservoirs can be seen , and the small rectangle to the south of the city is Angkor Wat , the temple mausoleum of King Suryavarman II ( reigned 1113-1150 ) . PHOTO : SUPPLIED <p> I always read what Gwynne Dyer has to say . Recently , it was a sobering account of how the Trump regime has decimated the American National Centre for Atmospheric Research . Trump 's minion , Russell Vought , has described concerns over human-induced climate change as ' ' climate fanaticism ' ' . <p> Archaeology is the only discipline that can identify past instances of climate change , and then assess the impact on human societies , and I have been involved for some time in one such case , that of the civilisation of Angkor . <p> In 1590s , Portuguese missionaries encountered in northwest Cambodia , the remnants of a great stone city overtaken by the jungle . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Zhou Daguan lived there , and in his memoire he described a thriving city , teeming with life . <p> He watched a procession by King Indravarman III , riding a massive elephant and holding the sword of state . He noted the busy market , people bathing in the heat of the day and the number of great temple mausolea for ancestral kings . <p> What happened between 1296 and 1590 ? <p> Angkor was dependent on the rice harvest . Perennial streams issuing from the Kulen upland to the north were diverted into reservoirs , the biggest measuring 8 by 2 kilometres . Each had a temple in the centre that linked the king with the success of the rice harvest . Water was fed into the rice fields below the city to supplement dry episodes during the monsoon period rains . <p> We now know from tree rings that from about 1400 , there was a sharp deterioration in the vital monsoon climate . Very dry years interspersed with catastrophic rains . Forest clearance north of Angkor as the city expanded brought huge loads of sediment down @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and the rice withered . <p> The god kings of Angkor had for centuries been seen as intermediaries with the Hindu gods . When they died , they assumed a new name and joined their deities . Essentially , the kings built the reservoirs , and were responsible for the success of the rice harvest . For them , climate change was a catastrophe . <p> During the 15th century , life at Angkor , once the largest dispersed urban complex in the word , became untenable and the court moved to a new capital near the Mekong River . The jungle began to encroach on the abandoned city and the first Portuguese came to ruins as they said , punctuated only by the sound of wild animals .

But I still get emotional . It was totally overwhelming for someone to say they 're going to do that for you . How do you even express how you feel knowing that someone is going to save your life ? The rare , inherited condition causes excess protein to accumulate in the kidneys and eventually leads to irreversible renal failure . <p> According to Kidney Health New Zealand , one in 10 people globally live with chronic kidney disease , often without realising until organ function has deteriorated significantly . Brigid is one of 10 siblings and chronic kidney disease has had a wide-reaching impact on the family . One sister received a transplant in her late thirties , passing away in her early fifties . <h> Family history and genetic testing <p> Her father , Jack , died without a diagnosis , but subsequent testing revealed the gene mutation runs through the paternal line , and many relatives have since tested positive for the gene , including Isabella 's mother Winnie . <p> " We 're lucky as a family because developments in science mean the disease can be linked to a particular gene , " shares Isabella , who did n't hesitate to get tested . <p> Advertisement <p> " I am the kind of person who prefers to know . Early detection is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disease , and I encourage other family members on the fence that this can be a positive step in terms of delaying onset . " <p> Isabella tested negative and explains testing positive is not a guarantee you will get the disease . <h> Life after testing <p> For Brigid , a devoted mental health nurse , there were decades where kidney disease did n't dramatically affect her life . She could still work , exercise and socialise . <p> " My kidneys deteriorated very slowly over a long period , " says Brigid , who in 2017 started on nightly dialysis as she slept . <h> The life-changing diagnosis <p> Then in 2019 , she was shocked to learn she needed a kidney transplant to survive . <p> " I never thought it would happen , " she admits . <p> " I 'm an optimistic person . I believed that it was going to be fine and I 'd keep living the way I was . " <p> Advertisement <p> Multiple family members stepped forward to be a donor but , for various reasons , were n't able @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a year , Isabella quietly went through the extensive screening process , not wanting to get anyone 's hopes up . Only when the testing reached a stage requiring both donor and recipient to be involved did she finally tell Brigid . <p> Isabella , then 25 , insists she simply did what anyone would . But Brigid disagrees . <p> She shares , " I will contradict Issy and say she is a remarkable person with an amazing heart , and is always giving . I do n't agree with her that just anybody would do that . " <h> Dedication to public health <p> " Bid has an incredible optimism and resilience , and continues to dedicate her life to the public health system , " shares Isabella . <p> After initially post-poning her Rhodes Scholar placement to donate her kidney , Isabella is now completing a PhD in climate change and health at the University of Oxford . She has travelled to Ghana for her Masters to research how kidney disease effects women there and she is also a qualified lawyer . <p> " I live a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ There are lots of things I want to do and I wo n't let one kidney stop me . C per decade between 1970 and 2015 to about 0.35 ? C per decade over the past 10 years . C ( 2.7F ) limit of the Paris agreement before 2030 , " said Stefan Rahmstorf , a scientist at the Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research and co-author of the study . <p> Extreme heat in recent years has been pushed higher by natural fluctuations -- such as solar cycles , volcanic eruptions , and the weather pattern El Ni ? o -- that have led scientists to question whether startling temperature readings are outliers or the result of an increase in global heating . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effect of nonhuman factors in five major datasets that scientists have compiled to gauge the Earth 's temperature . Photo : DoC <p> The snowy tops of the Southern Alps are a scenic backdrop southerners have had the luxury of taking for granted for generations . <p> Those who know this alpine landscape best , however , describe what is happening up here now as confronting and dangerous . <p> Until recent times , the alp 's snowcap has receded and advanced a little each season in fairly predictable patterns , albeit with an overall gradual decline in permanent ice . <h> read more <p> Today , tourists still view the scene in awe as they fly past every day but on the ground there is a changed landscape . Foot access to valley floors and alpine huts is a whole lot trickier and the thump of helicopter blades more frequent . <p> University of Canterbury glaciologist Dr Heather Purdie says retreating glaciers are more fissured making them convoluted to traverse @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their wake , newly exposed moraine forms great mounds of jumbled rocks -- a landscape so eroded in some places it is preventing the Department of Conservation from keeping public tracks open . <p> Freezing levels and snow lines have now crept so high up the mountainsides that large chunks of many glaciers , and even entire ancient bodies of ice , will soon sit permanently below them , no longer sustained by accumulating snow . <p> As a result , the sport of alpine mountaineering -- part of New Zealand 's culture and identity -- is becoming more dangerous , and accessible to fewer people . Lower down too , the days of tourists or the average New Zealander being able to get up-close and personal with a glacier and experience its dramatic icy-blue beauty and chill , are gone . <p> In Aoraki/Mt Cook National Park , traditional foot access to lower and medium-level locations along all glaciers has become more difficult with ice recession , says DoC 's Aoraki principal ranger , Dave Dittmer . <p> The resulting " rough landscape " makes for slower going on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through . <p> " It is so changeable and large , it is not possible to maintain any trail or route along moraines on the glaciers . " <p> He says DoC 's own alpine huts are not currently impacted with glacial recession in the park but in some valleys , the lower foot-access terrain leading to them is " significantly more difficult and challenging " . <p> " Access along both the lower Hooker and Tasman Glaciers is difficult , for example access to the Copland Pass up the Hooker Valley and access past Ball Hut towards Beetham Hut via the Reay Stream on the Malte Brun Range . " <p> Ball Hut , back in the day , could be reached in the comfort of an old landrover with tramper 's packs bouncing in the back , then it became foot-access only and now , with further erosion , even that is a challenge . <p> Higher up , snowy-white glaciers traditionally provided a safe and magnificent pathway to peaks , huts and bivvies , as well as stabilising steep valley sides . <p> " Last week the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Valley . " Dittmer says , " This hut was located on a rock ridge that was slowly sliding downwards due to the Murchison Glacier recession . " <p> The hut , owned by the club , had been officially deemed unfit to stay in since 2017 . <p> Dittmer says hikers need to do their research before visiting the area and even contact the rangers for the latest advice . <h> True story <p> For scientists , glaciers are the canary in a coal mine for climate change . They react quickly to any fluctuations and as glaciologists the world over say ... they do n't tell lies . <p> Purdie , who has been monitoring New Zealand 's glaciers for the past two decades , has just returned from yet another end-of-summer data-collection trip to measure ice at Rolleston Glacier . This is the 17th year this glacier , regarded as a benchmark for the World Glacier Monitoring Service , has been measured . <p> It sits high in a cirque -- a bowl-shaped valley -- and has lost around 11m in height since the study began . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ straight onto the glacier from its rocky margin ; now they scramble down the rocks to reach the ice surface . <p> The temperature can be as much as 1degC warmer per 200m drop in altitude . As the tops of glaciers like Rolleston melt lower , they sit in a warmer place and the newly exposed rock around them crumbles . The resulting dust coats their tops leading to more heat absorption , and at the same time crevasses form and are exposed , creating internal pouches of warmer air . <p> " Some of the most dramatic change I have observed is at the Fox Glacier . Fox Glacier has undergone very rapid thinning and shortening . It is becoming increasingly debris ( rock ) covered and broken on its lower tongue . " <p> For Purdie , who has had a life-long connection with these beautiful , brutal places , seeing the melt accelerate so quickly is unsettling . <p> " Even as a scientist who has been monitoring glaciers in New Zealand for over 20 years , the rate of change is still really confronting . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be able to experience the glaciers . I used to work as a glacier guide at Fox Glacier ; we could still take walking trips up the valley , so trips that families could go on , now all the glacier-hiking trips require helicopter access , which means that many people will not get the opportunity to walk on the ice . " <p> She would like to see solutions that enabled foot access to continue . <p> Rolleston Glacier is lowering as the snowline creeps up in the Southern Alps . Photo : Heather Purdie <p> " One thing that concerns me from a recreational perspective is the loss of walking access as agencies tasked with managing backcountry infrastructure are tending to see helicopters as the answer to deteriorating access -- this is a real shame . Last year I was in Nepal and despite their glaciers looking much like ours -- they still support ground trails , bridges , tracks in the mountains -- people still walk everywhere . It would be nice if we were to focus on supporting low-carbon access into the New Zealand mountains @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ huts . " <p> He 's observed the physical changes , and worries that planning for how the altering landscape is managed has fallen off the radar . <p> " Planning is not keeping pace with the changes . Things like access issues and different opportunities at Aoraki Mt Cook -- it 's all becoming very limited there now , that 's why you 're ending up with this cul-de-sac at either Mueller Hut or the Hooker Valley . " <p> He says the same situation existed on the Westland side where park plans compiled long ago for experiencing features like large glaciers were no longer relevant due to dramatic change . <p> " Back then there was a bit of talk about the glaciers moving forward and back , a bit of thought that they might go backwards but nothing like what has happened . " <p> He says four huts in the Aoraki area have been either moved or removed due to damage in the past five years or so , mainly from rock falls and subsidence caused by glacial recession in the valleys below them . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ went to Aoraki Mt Cook in 1988 the lake at the end of the Tasman Glacier was small . " <p> It 's been 30 years since Anderson began observing glaciers and over that period Franz Joesph 's once-magnificent great ice mass had advanced about 500m but retreated by 2.5km . <p> At Brewster , landscape changes had also been dramatic with the solid head of the glacier now broken up into four or five pieces that no longer directly fed the main ice mass . <p> " I remember years ago trying to balance on a ledge between the glacier and the lake to get samples . @@135840461 <p> University of Auckland research fellow Louise Wilson in the Hauraki Gulf , which is protected from some types of fishing , but not from other kinds of human impact . Photo : Paul Caiger <p> Expert opinion from University of Auckland - Waipapa Taumata Rau <p> The Hauraki Gulf is doing it tough . This summer , shearwaters struggled to find enough food to feed their chicks , and korora/little blue penguins washed up malnourished . This marine region -- considered a pataka kai , or food cupboard -- is under extreme stress . <p> Some of the reasons for this stress are large and complex -- marine heatwaves , climate change , the long-term effects of dredging and overfishing . But one of them is very simple : human activity makes a lot of noise underwater . <p> Noise is stressful , but it also makes it more difficult for fish and other marine species to communicate with each other , to find places to settle down and breed , or to avoid predators . Even seagrass is harmed by too much noise . Over time , stress @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tend to live shorter lives and have fewer offspring . <h> read more <p> Parts of the Hauraki Gulf , as well as other marine areas around New Zealand , are protected from some types of fishing , but not from other kinds of human impact . " Sound is a big one , " says Craig Radford , a professor of marine science at Waipapa Taumata Rau , University of Auckland . " We could really make a huge impact in terms of how we design marine reserves . " <h> Why sound is crucial in the ocean <p> Sound travels extremely well through water , so marine species use it to communicate and sense their surroundings . They use it in the same way that we rely on light to perceive the world around us . <p> On a clear day , you might be able to see all the way to the horizon . Whales and dolphins can hear sounds all the way to the horizon and beyond -- no matter what time of day it is . " Sound works even in the dark , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ equally . It 's not affected by water currents and things like that . So , it really is quite a widely used sensory cue by a whole range of different animals under the sea . " <p> At least , they 're trying to use it . Increasingly , human sounds drown out whatever fish and crustaceans and dolphins and whales are trying to say . " That 's why we 're really interested in noise effects , " says Radford . " The world is getting noisier , and it 's the same underwater . " <p> Noise affects not only communication : larvae floating in the water column listen for the sound of reefs to find somewhere to live , orienting themselves towards the chorus from other animals there . <h> What happens when things get too loud ? <p> Loud noises close to animals can physically harm them , says University of Auckland research fellow Louise Wilson , who co-authored the paper . " There are studies that have shown sound pollution can rupture the swim bladder of fishes . They can also experience either permanent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is ' masking , ' otherwise known as the cocktail party effect : a reduction in the distance over which species can communicate . " Just like being at a party , right ? " says Radford , " If you want to talk to someone and it 's a loud party , you 've got to be really close together . " <p> If the sound drowns out that animal 's natural hearing range , it makes it harder for it to avoid predators , find prey , or breed . " And that has implications for the survival of that animal , " says Wilson . " If an animal is having to spend more time looking for food and less time resting , that 's less time that they 're able to spend breeding -- and can have knock-on effects for fitness and survival . " <p> Various types of marine protection ban fishing from certain areas but do n't put any restrictions on cruise ships motoring through the middle of them . " You ca n't fish , but you can drive an oil tanker through @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ships in general are really loud , he says . Small boats are loud but quick . Large boats are loud but slow . And in the Hauraki Gulf , there can be a lot of them . <p> The scientists know what happens when the boats are absent . During the Covid lockdowns , University of Auckland hydrophones recorded a momentous change in the seascape . The number of marine species that were audible increased dramatically . They heard seals , fish , invertebrates , Bryde 's whales , dolphins . " We showed that when you reduce the impact of pleasure craft , all of a sudden the range the animals can hear expands , " says Radford . " So that was quite serendipitous and quite cool . " <p> The communication range of fish and dolphins increased by an estimated 65 percent , according to a paper Radford , Wilson and colleagues published about their lockdown research . With greater communication , the natural behaviours of sea life have a chance to return . <h> It 's a problem , but with a simple fix <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has been modified or changed over the past few years -- they could consider the impacts of noise . " You can just do things as simple as having quiet zones , or restricting speed to reduce noise , " says Radford . <p> Even considering the amount of noisy recreational activity that 's allowed to happen in a marine protected area would be a start , says Wilson . " Or simply limiting it at specific times of year when you know from existing research that specific species which use sound are going to be more vulnerable to sound pollution , " she says . <p> There are currently a few noise restrictions in the sea so far , particularly where construction is concerned . Marine construction , mining , and surveying all involve a noise-impact assessment , " which is relatively new for New Zealand , " says Radford . <p> But we do n't consider the noise impact of ships travelling 24/7 through our waters . Or other craft : Radford points out that helicopter pads along the coast can lead to a great deal of noise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ drones and airplanes : all audible underwater . <p> To save our seas , the researchers are clear : human-made sound is a factor that should be an important part of the criteria deciding marine protection legislation . <p> The world is facing unprecedented environmental challenges . Planetary Solutions , an initiative of the Sustainability Hub at Waipapa Taumata Rau , University of Auckland , and Newsroom , explores these issues -- and the practical ways we can all be part of the solution .

But the facts tell a different story . It was who got blamed and who got policed . That 's why in August , 2021 , the Labour-led government offered Pacific communities a formal apology for the Dawn Raids arising from discriminatory immigration laws . Many would argue that the raids are an example of racialised state violence rather than neutral immigration control . <p> Fast forward to today , and the language may be softer , but the script is similar . When politicians invoke ' overstayers ' and put pressure on public services , they risk reactivating a narrative that casts Pacific people as a burden , even when evidence does not support that framing . <p> At the same time , Luxon offers a solution through a ' liberalised ' approach to immigration that offers more flexible pathways . On the surface , this sounds inclusive , but in practice , liberalised systems tend to prioritise skills , qualifications and economic capital . These criteria systematically advantage applicants from wealthier countries . <p> New Zealand 's immigration settings have long privileged education systems deemed comparable to our own , particularly those based on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , Ireland and parts of Europe benefit from easier visa pathways and qualification recognition , while applicants from many Pacific nations face additional hurdles around documentation , income thresholds and credential equivalency . <p> For example , people from Pacific nations such as Tuvalu and Kiribati who are being pushed to migrate because of climate change and disasters must still meet age thresholds and ' acceptable health ' criteria . This effectively excludes those with disabilities or medical conditions deemed too costly . <p> What Luxon 's Government and those before it fail to recognise is that Pacific mobility to Aotearoa is not new , nor is it simply economics . Pacific migration is grounded in long-standing historical , cultural and political relationships . <p> Scotty Morrison 's ' Origins ' series provides excellent insights into how Maori and Pacific peoples share deep genealogical and navigational connections across Te Moana-nui-a-Kiwa . The series traces ancestral voyaging routes and cultural connections , reinforcing that these relationships are not recent or incidental . <p> Moreover , large-scale migration from places like Samoa , Tonga and the Cook Islands accelerated after World War @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fill workforce shortages in manufacturing and infrastructure . By the 1970s , Pacific communities were integral to New Zealand 's economy and society , <p> Reducing Pacific migration to visa categories and risk profiles ignores that history . Pacific peoples are not just another group of applicants in a global labour market . This approach overlooks whakapapa , kinship , and shared identity across the region . <p> Supporters of liberalisation might argue that a more flexible system benefits everyone , creates opportunities , and fills skill shortages . And there is some truth to that . New Zealand does need an immigration system that is responsive and effective . <p> However , a system that appears neutral but consistently advantages already privileged groups is not equitable . It simply masks inequality behind the language of efficiency . <p> If the Government is serious about its ' Pacific family ' rhetoric , it needs to move beyond symbolic language and confront these structural realities . That could mean rethinking visa settings for Pacific nations , expanding genuine mobility pathways , and acknowledging the unique historical and relational ties that distinguish @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @

@@135859412 <p> New Zealand 's response to the oil shocks of 1973 and 1978 was a series of poor political decisions , called Think Big . Mostly , it was an expensive disaster . <p> Today we are contemplating a new series of decisions which , even if there was no war in Iran , are similarly foolish . <p> The child of Think Big is to hand . <p> The first foolish decision is to spend $1billion building a LNG ( liquefied natural gas ) terminal because we fear running out of gas . Yet if Methanex stopped making and exporting methanol , there would be no gas shortage . <p> Methanex traces its origins back to the Think Big era and is easily our largest gas consumer , using about 40% of New Zealand 's gas . <p> Imported LNG costs are about double the cost of redirected Methanex gas , even before the Iran war arrived . <p> Why would our government ever think it a good idea to leave that absurdity unchallenged ? <p> We have been there before . <p> As Minister of Energy about 20 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ contracts because the field was starting to run down even then . <p> The result was that Methanex took a less dominant role in the market and electricity generation took a more dominant role . <p> Negotiations took time , every Thursday evening for several months as I recall . <p> But the taxpayer paid nothing and no-one went to court . <p> The gas industry accommodated these transitions well , not least because Jim Bolger , then retired from politics , accepted my request that he chair the newly created gas company . <p> Surely , we can do that again . <p> It is now time for Methanex to exit the New Zealand economy altogether . Methanex gas has a much more strategic purpose than being squandered to produce and export methanol . <p> The second foolish decision is to abandon the feasibility study for the Lake Onslow pumped hydro project . <p> This project is designed to address our biggest domestic energy risk -- a dry winter . <p> That risk is set to grow , because our economy is progressively electrifying . Today electricity is about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rises to say 50% , or even 60% , our risk during a dry winter also grows proportionally . <p> However , the risk of a dry winter is increasing for another reason . <p> Solar power prices have plummeted in recent years and further drops are certain . <p> It is now New Zealand 's cheapest and fastest growing source of electricity . <p> But winters are dark , so the new threat is a dry , dark winter . <p> The government abandoned the Lake Onslow feasibility study with a lot of petulant rhetoric and shamefully misleading comment on costs . Fortunately , some rather clever private sector folk ignored all that . They have seen the opportunity and have stepped in to refine earlier progress . <p> They are preparing a consent application , now being fast-tracked and are sure they will find cost savings as they go . <p> It is hard to see how the project might not stack up , given our whole economy is on the line in a dry year . But now we are at least going to find out , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ensure we do n't have blackouts in coming years while we sort all this stuff out ? The answer is coal . <p> There is a stockpile of coal at our largest power station in Huntly . The coal can just sit there , for years , unless or until we need it . <p> Of course , coal is filthy and produces a lot of carbon dioxide , but it is a legitimate short-term backstop if the alternative is a blackout . <p> The Huntly station is approaching the end of its life , so unlike a brand-new LNG terminal there is no capital cost to recover . Paradoxically , using coal in the next few years to ensure security of supply will hasten the economy 's switch from coal , oil and gas to electricity . <p> Companies considering the switch to electricity will have more confidence that they wo n't be caught short in a dry winter . <p> It makes strategic sense for New Zealand to abandon plans for a LNG terminal now and instead swing some attention in the direction of long-term solutions to electricity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have to go cold turkey in a decade or two . <p> Climate change is not going away and the impacts here and abroad all point unswervingly in the direction of decarbonising . <p> But what of electricity prices ? Investment in a facility such as Lake Onslow would immediately provide opportunity for new generator entrants to buy storage , or ' ' firming ' ' . That would mean more competition in the market .

" <p> Grenon , a private equity investor originally from Calgary who moved here in 2012 , is the largest individual shareholder in media company NZME , owner of the Herald and Newstalk ZB , and a director since leading a partial boardroom spill a year ago . <p> He campaigned for a cleanout of the board by advocating for his own hands-on direction of the business , cost cuts in the executive and middle management ranks , clearer financial reporting and ' balance ' in the journalism of the Herald . Grenon now owns 19.9 percent of the NZME shares after investing more than $30m , raising his holding as late as last week . <p> He has funded the alt-media website and newsletter the Centrist , which carries right-leaning content on matters from the Treaty to the pandemic , gender issues to climate change , economic freedoms to wokeness and conspiracy theories . <p> Legally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ law and has a long history of taking fights all the way to the Supreme Court of Canada on personal and business issues . <p> In New Zealand , he was last week revealed as the inspiration and funder of a " wholly unsuccessful " defamation case taken by Julian Batchelor against TVNZ over claims of anti-Maori racism . <p> Stuff reports Batchelor had organised a " stop co-governance " roadshow and sued TVNZ and a disinformation project researcher Sanjana Hattotuwa over an August 2023 news item about an investigation into a pamphlet Batchelor distributed pre-election . <p> Batchelor revealed in court that Grenon was not only the funder of his case but had contacted him to suggest that he sue . <p> In finding against Batchelor , Judge David Clark raised questions : <p> " The first is the question of who is the real plaintiff in this proceeding ? The second goes to the question of costs . If Mr Grenon is paying the legal costs of Mr Batchelor , then what exposure does Mr Grenon have to an adverse costs award against Mr Batchelor ? " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ presence in the proceedings was unknown " until Batchelor was required by the Court to answer the question about who was covering his legal fees , Stuff reported . <p> " But rather than this having any influence on my determinations of the merits of the claim , it is an issue which will go to costs , particularly when , as I commented during the hearing , Mr Grenon has not had an opportunity to address the Court . " <p> The judge found the claim to have been " wholly unsuccessful " and set a telephone conference in April for a discussion on whether Grenon would want to be heard on any costs order . <p> Grenon would not comment to Newsroom on his involvement in the Batchelor vs TVNZ case .
